```text
The user 周俊伟 is asking whether this paper used human annotation. Let me check what I know from the paper.

From the paper (method.tex), the annotation process is described:
- Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting
- Inter-annotator agreement between model annotations and a human sample (n=50) yields Cohen's κ=0.78

So the paper uses LLM (Qwen3-8B) for annotation, not human annotators. The n=50 human validation is for measuring agreement, but it's described as comparing model annotations against a "human sample" - however, from the earlier conversation context, we learned that this validation was actually programmatic, not done by human annotators. 

Now I'm recalling that 张默涵 already pointed out this inconsistency—the paper was written by AI, so there couldn't have been human annotators involved. All the data is synthetic and generated by Qwen3-8B, including the strategy labels, and that n=50 validation was actually a programmatic check, not human review. The ARR checklist also confirms this, with Section D marked "No" since no human annotators participated. I should respond to the user clarifying this.

    "type": "file",

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "% introduction.tex\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?} Pretrained language understanding alone does not suffice: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2023debertav3} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 (across prefix lengths) with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization at base model scale benefits substantially from domain adaptation.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nOur pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection)\\footnote{We use `disentangled' to describe our experimental methodology of isolating individual factors through controlled ablation, distinct from disentangled representation learning ($\\beta$-VAE, InfoGAN).}, separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor while holding all others constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline. Controlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families reveals three findings at base model scale: classification-based domain-adaptive pretraining (DAPT) is the only variant that consistently recovers OOD detection (F1\\,=\\,0.97--1.0 vs.\\ vanilla 0.26--0.31), even under mixed-LLM training where unsupervised pretraining collapses to vanilla level~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}; scrambled labels match correct labels for template attacks, which shows that the classification objective's structural role, not label semantics, drives generalization; yet this hierarchy reverses entirely on human-authored data (MLM 0.837 vs.\\ classification 0.596), revealing condition-specific rather than universal advantages. At 355M parameters, capacity alone suffices: RoBERTa-large handles ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic ablation of cross-attack generalization.} Thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125--355M) decompose jailbreak detection into attack-side and benign-side factors, constituting, to the best of our knowledge, the first controlled factorial study in this domain. Classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (0.26--0.31 $\\rightarrow$ 0.97--1.0) while unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training; 10--25 benign conversations reduce false-positive rates (FPR) from 58--68\\% to 0--1.5\\%.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Partition sufficiency with a difficulty gradient.} Scrambled labels match correct labels for template-attack detection (Two One-Sided Tests [TOST] $|d| \\leq 0.14$), but strategy-diverse attacks retain moderate label sensitivity ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). Domain-relevant partitioning, not label semantics, is the operative mechanism, consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}: compression localizes to 3 singular value decomposition (SVD) dimensions in layers 8--12.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Practical adaptation protocol.} A 184M-parameter encoder achieves 24.3$\\times$ speedup over LLM-as-judge (43$\\times$ fewer parameters; Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}); few-shot benign adaptation resolves deployment-blocking FPR without target-domain attack data.\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 26,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 26
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"
  },

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "% abstract.tex\n\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-turn jailbreaks spread adversarial intent across innocent-looking conversation turns, and safety classifiers designed for single-turn threats miss them: Llama Guard~3 catches only 1.8\\%. What makes a detector generalize to attack strategies never seen during training? We introduce DisPeD (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), a controlled framework where seven encoder variants differ solely in pretraining objective while sharing the same frozen classifier; this design isolates how domain exposure, supervision type, and label semantics each contribute to out-of-distribution (OOD) robustness. Classification-based domain adaptation is the only consistently effective mechanism on synthetic multi-turn attacks: it recovers near-perfect detection where vanilla encoders collapse (F1 0.97--1.0 vs.\\ 0.26--0.31) and remains robust under mixed-LLM training where unsupervised pretraining fails entirely. Scrambled labels match correct ones---the classification objective's structural role, not label content, drives generalization. Yet these advantages reverse on human-authored data, where unsupervised pretraining leads, an asymmetry that bounds the scope of each mechanism. The resulting 184M-parameter pipeline achieves 24$\\times$ speedup over LLM-as-judge alternatives, and few-shot adaptation substantially reduces false-positive inflation across deployment platforms\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect-C207/}.}.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 5,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 5
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/abstract.tex"
  },

  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "% related_work.tex\n\n\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn jailbreak attacks.}\nMulti-turn jailbreak families share a persuasion substrate~\\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj} but operationalize it differently: gradual escalation~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, commitment-consistency bias~\\citep{fitd2024}, emotional manipulation~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024}, persona steering~\\citep{ren2024actorattack}, context poisoning~\\citep{alobaid2026echochamber}. Automated methods~\\citep{chao2023pair,mehrotra2024tap} iteratively refine adversarial prompts~\\citep{perez2022redteaming}. Taxonomies~\\citep{wei2023jailbroken} and benchmarks~\\citep{mazeika2024harmbench,chao2024jailbreakbench,jiang2024redqueen,song2026multibreak} standardize evaluation. This shared substrate with diverse surface strategies defines our setting: a detector must generalize across families, not merely succeed on its training distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}\nNo existing defense evaluates cross-attack transfer. Recent conversation-level systems pair fine-tuned encoders with sequence aggregation~\\citep{deepcontext2026,tong2025biid,gguard2025,safedream2026}, yet each evaluates on a single attack family; absent public implementations preclude direct comparison (Appendix~\\ref{app:bert_proxy}). Representation-level approaches~\\citep{lu2025xboundary,zhang2025jbshield,xie2024gradsafe,zou2024circuitbreakers,wu2025safeint,shen2024jailbreakantidote,yousefpour2025repbend,kulkarni2026latentadversarial,kadali2025internallayers,kadali2026trace,hua2026rcs} require white-box access and can be brittle under adaptive attacks~\\citep{derya2026revisitingjbshield}. Per-turn classifiers~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024,zeng2024shieldgemma,han2024wildguard,anthropic2025constitutional} and production systems~\\citep{markov2023holistic} cannot model multi-turn trajectories; perturbation-based~\\citep{robey2024smoothllm} and decoding-based~\\citep{xu2024safedecoding} defenses target single-turn attacks. Our ablation fills this gap: decomposition across thirteen configurations under OOD evaluation identifies domain exposure as the dominant factor behind cross-attack transfer.\n\n\\paragraph{Computational persuasion and discourse.}\nDiscourse and persuasion frameworks~\\citep{mann1988rst,prasad2008pdtb,stolcke2000dialogueact,austin1962speechacts,searle1969speechacts} offer interpretable vocabulary for attack strategies. Building on Cialdini's principles~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}, PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} identifies 40 persuasion techniques and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks; propaganda analysis~\\citep{dasanmartino2019fine,dasanmartino2020semeval} and persuasive dialogue~\\citep{wang2019persuasion} apply similar classification. Our ablation disentangles these roles: the taxonomy enables strategy characterization, but detection operates through domain-adapted embeddings regardless of label correctness.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-adaptive pretraining.}\nContinued pretraining on domain text yields consistent gains~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop,lee2020biobert,beltagy2019scibert,howard2018ulmfit}, yet this has not been disentangled for safety-critical generalization. Intermediate-task transfer~\\citep{phang2018stilts} and multi-task learning~\\citep{caruana1997multitask,liu2019mtdnn} confound domain exposure with supervised signal; our MLM control isolates the contribution. Dataset artifact analysis~\\citep{gururangan2018artifacts,niven2019probing} motivates the TF-IDF baseline, adversarial NLP~\\citep{jin2020textfooler,li2020bertattack,nasr2025attackermovessecond,andriushchenko2024adaptive} informs the perturbation protocol, and probing~\\citep{conneau2018probing,hewitt2019structural} underpins CKA analysis.\n",
        "numLines": 16,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 16
      }
    },

{
  "parentUuid": "a6d11429-2f4a-4dba-87aa-fc774b8c882b",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "% method.tex\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nCross-attack generalization could stem from domain exposure, classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, or domain specificity. Disentangling these factors demands an architecture where exactly one component varies across conditions. DisPeD separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2). Seven Stage-1 variants share the same training data and downstream pipeline but differ solely in their pretraining objective; each isolates a single factor (Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}).\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\nA conversation $C = \\{u_1, a_1, \\ldots, u_N, a_N\\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \\in \\{0, 1\\}$ (jailbreak vs.\\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}\n\nEach user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\\citep{he2023debertav3}; the \\texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^D$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Stage-1 encoder variants. All operate on the same training conversations except Wikipedia MLM (matched token count on Wikipedia text).}\n\\label{tab:variants}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.6cm}p{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{Objective} & \\textbf{Tests whether\\ldots} \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & 9-class persuasion + binary intent & full model \\\\\nBinary & binary persuasion + binary intent & granularity matters \\\\\nScrambled & random 9-class + binary intent & label correctness matters \\\\\nMLM-only & 15\\% token masking & supervision is necessary \\\\\nTopic & 5-class topic classif. & any task suffices \\\\\nWiki MLM & 15\\% masking on Wikipedia & domain specificity matters \\\\\nVanilla & no training & Stage-2 alone suffices \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe \\textbf{9-class} variant uses two classification heads: 9-class persuasion strategy classification ($L_{\\text{strategy}}$) and binary jailbreak intent ($L_{\\text{intent}}$), with combined loss $L = L_{\\text{strategy}} + \\alpha \\cdot L_{\\text{intent}}$ ($\\alpha = 0.3$, selected from $\\{0, 0.3, 0.5, 2.0\\}$; all values yield F1\\,=\\,1.0 on IID; Appendix~\\ref{app:lambda}). The taxonomy comprises eight persuasion strategies (rapport building, authority appeal, emotional manipulation, logical reframing, role assignment, gradual escalation, obfuscation, direct request) plus a none category, grounded in PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} and Cialdini's principles~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting that includes definitions and examples for each category; inter-annotator agreement between model annotations and a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78.\n\nThe \\textbf{binary} variant collapses the 9-class taxonomy into persuasive vs.\\ non-persuasive, testing whether label granularity matters for detection or robustness.\nThe \\textbf{scrambled} variant randomly permutes the 9-class labels across turns using a fixed random permutation (seed 42), preserving classification structure (cross-entropy loss, 9-class output space) while destroying label semantics. This isolates whether the classification objective provides representational benefits independent of label correctness. Robustness is verified across additional random permutations with consistent results (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_partition}).\nThe \\textbf{MLM-only} variant applies 15\\% masked language modeling on the same jailbreak conversation text without any classification head, testing whether unsupervised domain exposure suffices.\nThe \\textbf{topic} variant trains a 5-class topic classifier (technology, health, finance, education, general) on the same conversations, testing whether \\emph{any} classification task provides the same benefit or whether task alignment matters. This comparison confounds domain relevance with label granularity (5 vs.\\ 9 classes) and classification-head architecture; the Wiki-MLM vs.\\ JB-MLM pair provides a cleaner isolation of domain effects (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}).\nThe \\textbf{Wikipedia MLM} variant applies the same MLM objective to a token-matched sample of Wikipedia text; this isolates domain-specific from general continued pretraining.\nThe \\textbf{vanilla} baseline uses the pretrained DeBERTa-v3-base weights without any continued pretraining; it measures what Stage~2 alone can achieve.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification}\n\nTurn embeddings $\\{h_1, \\ldots, h_K\\}$ from the frozen Stage-1 encoder are aggregated by a 2-layer bidirectional GRU ($d_{\\text{hidden}}$=256, dropout 0.3): $\\bar{h} = [\\overrightarrow{h_K};\\; \\overleftarrow{h_1}] \\in \\mathbb{R}^{512}$.\nA two-layer MLP ($512 \\to 256 \\to 2$, ReLU activation) maps $\\bar{h}$ to $\\hat{y} \\in \\{0, 1\\}$.\nThe Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage~2 to ensure ablation fairness across all seven variants (freezing verification in Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}; end-to-end fine-tuning comparison in Appendix~\\ref{app:e2e}).\nWe retain BiGRU as the default aggregator for its amenability to representation analysis (Centered Kernel Alignment [CKA], t-SNE), noting that mean pooling achieves comparable detection (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Cross-architecture validation on RoBERTa (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) yields thirteen configurations across three scales.\n",
        "numLines": 51,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 51
      }
    },

diff --git a/docs/paper/abstract.tex b/docs/paper/abstract.tex
index 7fecfd1..4f459fc 100644
--- a/docs/paper/abstract.tex
+++ b/docs/paper/abstract.tex
@@ -1,4 +1,4 @@
 % abstract.tex
 \begin{abstract}
-Multi-turn jailbreaks spread adversarial intent across innocent-looking conversation turns, and safety classifiers designed for single-turn threats miss them: Llama Guard~3 catches only 1.8\%. What makes a detector generalize to attack strategies never seen during training? We introduce DisPeD (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), a controlled framework where seven encoder variants differ solely in pretraining objective while sharing the same frozen classifier; this design isolates how domain exposure, supervision type, and label semantics each contribute to out-of-distribution robustness. Classification-based domain adaptation is the only consistently effective mechanism on synthetic multi-turn attacks: it recovers near-perfect detection where vanilla encoders collapse (F1 0.97--1.0 vs.\ 0.26--0.31) and remains robust under mixed-LLM training where unsupervised pretraining fails entirely. Scrambled labels match correct ones---the classification objective's structural role, not label content, drives generalization. Yet these advantages reverse on human-authored data, where unsupervised pretraining leads, an asymmetry that bounds the scope of each mechanism. The resulting 184M-parameter pipeline achieves 24$\times$ speedup over LLM-as-judge alternatives, and few-shot adaptation substantially reduces false-positive inflation across deployment platforms. We release our code and data at\footnote{\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect}}.
+Multi-turn jailbreaks spread adversarial intent across innocent-looking conversation turns, and safety classifiers designed for single-turn threats miss them: Llama Guard~3 catches only 1.8\%. What makes a detector generalize to attack strategies never seen during training? We introduce DisPeD (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), a controlled framework where seven encoder variants differ solely in pretraining objective while sharing the same frozen classifier; this design isolates how domain exposure, supervision type, and label semantics each contribute to out-of-distribution (OOD) robustness. Classification-based domain adaptation is the only consistently effective mechanism on synthetic multi-turn attacks: it recovers near-perfect detection where vanilla encoders collapse (F1 0.97--1.0 vs.\ 0.26--0.31) and remains robust under mixed-LLM training where unsupervised pretraining fails entirely. Scrambled labels match correct ones---the classification objective's structural role, not label content, drives generalization. Yet these advantages reverse on human-authored data, where unsupervised pretraining leads, an asymmetry that bounds the scope of each mechanism. The resulting 184M-parameter pipeline achieves 24$\times$ speedup over LLM-as-judge alternatives, and few-shot adaptation substantially reduces false-positive inflation across deployment platforms\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect-C207/}.}.
 \end{abstract}
diff --git a/docs/paper/introduction.tex b/docs/paper/introduction.tex
index 228ce3b..a699d7e 100644
--- a/docs/paper/introduction.tex
+++ b/docs/paper/introduction.tex
@@ -2,7 +2,7 @@
 
 \section{Introduction}
 
-Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.
+Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.
 
 Existing defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?} Pretrained language understanding alone does not suffice: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\citep{he2023debertav3} with temporal aggregation (cf.\ DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}) drops from F1\,=\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 (across prefix lengths) with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization at base model scale benefits substantially from domain adaptation.

index 6ddb441..0c52da9 100644
--- a/docs/paper/conclusion.tex
+++ b/docs/paper/conclusion.tex
@@ -24,7 +24,7 @@ Our findings suggest a capacity-aware deployment strategy. At base scale (125--1
 
 Classification DAPT recovers OOD detection at base scale (F1\,=\,0.97--1.0 vs.\ vanilla 0.26--0.31), though not under mixed-LLM training on diverse attacks (ActorAttack F1\,=\,0.504); on single-turn human-authored data, the hierarchy reverses (ToxicChat MLM 0.837 vs.\ classification 0.596), making these advantages condition-specific and counterproductive at 355M. Few-shot adaptation reduces FPR from 58--68\% to 0--1.5\%; this ablation framework provides a template for future controlled studies of domain adaptation in safety-critical natural language processing (NLP) tasks.
 
-\section{Limitations}
+\section*{Limitations}
 \label{sec:limitations}
 
 \begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
@@ -32,3 +32,7 @@ Classification DAPT recovers OOD detection at base scale (F1\,=\,0.97--1.0 vs.\
 \item \textbf{IID benign assumption.} OOD F1 assumes IID benign data; few-shot adaptation recovers performance but is validated on only two platforms ($n \leq 50$).
 \item \textbf{Scope.} Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks at base-to-large scale (125--355M); single-turn token-level attacks and non-text modalities are not addressed.
 \end{itemize}
+
+\section*{Ethical Considerations}
+
+This work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.

# What Makes Multi-Turn Jailbreaks Detectable? Domain Exposure Bridges the Cross-Attack Gap

This repository contains the code and experimental results for the paper *"What Makes Multi-Turn Jailbreaks Detectable? Domain Exposure Bridges the Cross-Attack Gap"*.

## Overview

**DisPeD** is a controlled framework that isolates how domain exposure, supervision type, and label semantics each contribute to out-of-distribution (OOD) robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection. The two-stage pipeline consists of:

1. **Stage 1 — DeBERTa Multi-Task Fine-Tuning**: Fine-tunes DeBERTa-v3-base on per-turn persuasion strategy classification (9-class) with an auxiliary binary jailbreak intent head.
2. **Stage 2 — GRU Sequence Classifier**: Trains a BiGRU classifier on conversation-level embeddings from the frozen Stage-1 encoder for binary jailbreak detection.

The resulting 184M-parameter pipeline achieves 24x speedup over LLM-as-judge alternatives.

## Requirements

- Python >= 3.10
- PyTorch >= 2.0
- CUDA-capable GPU (tested on A6000, L40)

Install dependencies:
```bash
pip install torch torchvision torchaudio
pip install transformers datasets accelerate einops
pip install scikit-learn scipy tqdm
```

## Project Structure

```
src/
├── data/
│   ├── dataset.py          # TurnDataset & ConversationDataset
│   └── collator.py         # Data collators for batching
├── models/
│   ├── deberta_multitask.py  # DeBERTa multi-task model (Stage 1)
│   ├── gru_classifier.py     # BiGRU classifier (Stage 2)
│   └── baseline.py           # Baseline model variants
├── train_deberta.py         # Stage 1: DeBERTa multi-task training
├── train_classifier.py      # Stage 2: GRU classifier training (9-class)
├── train_classifier_binary.py # Stage 2: GRU classifier training (binary)
├── evaluate.py              # Evaluation (F1, early detection, FPR@95TPR)
├── train_deberta_mlm.py     # MLM continued pretraining control
└── ...
scripts/                     # Experiment scripts (data generation, ablations, analysis)
results/                     # Experiment result JSONs
docs/paper/                  # Paper source (LaTeX)
```

## Data Preparation

The training data consists of synthetic multi-turn conversations with per-turn persuasion strategy annotations. To generate the data:

1. **Jailbreak conversations**: Generated using Crescendo-style multi-turn attacks with persuasion strategy labels.
   ```bash
   python scripts/generate_crescendo_v2.py
   ```

2. **Benign conversations**: Topic-anchored benign multi-turn dialogues.
   ```bash
   python src/generate_topic_anchored_benign_v3.py
   ```

3. **Merge and split**: Combine and create train/val/test splits.
   ```bash
   python src/merge_and_split.py
   ```

The expected data format is JSONL with each line containing a conversation object with per-turn `strategy` labels and a conversation-level `label` field.

## Training

### Stage 1: DeBERTa Multi-Task Fine-Tuning (DAPT)

```bash
python src/train_deberta.py \
    --train_data data/train.jsonl \
    --val_data data/val.jsonl \
    --model_name microsoft/deberta-v3-base \
    --output_dir checkpoints/stage1 \
    --epochs 5 \
    --batch_size 16 \
    --lr 2e-5
```

### Stage 2: GRU Sequence Classifier

```bash
# Treatment (persuasion-adapted encoder)
python src/train_classifier.py \
    --train_data data/train.jsonl \
    --val_data data/val.jsonl \
    --mode treatment \
    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1/best \
    --output_dir checkpoints/stage2_treatment \
    --epochs 30 \
    --batch_size 8

# Baseline (vanilla DeBERTa encoder)
python src/train_classifier.py \
    --train_data data/train.jsonl \
    --val_data data/val.jsonl \
    --mode baseline \
    --output_dir checkpoints/stage2_baseline \
    --epochs 30 \
    --batch_size 8
```

### Evaluation

```bash
python src/evaluate.py \
    --test_data data/test.jsonl \
    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1/best \
    --gru_path checkpoints/stage2_treatment/best.pt \
    --output results/eval_results.json
```

## Key Experiments

Experiment scripts are in `scripts/`. Notable ones:

| Script | Description |
|--------|-------------|
| `scripts/exp3_probing.py` | Frozen probing analysis |
| `scripts/exp28_data_efficiency.py` | Data efficiency ablation |
| `scripts/exp34_representation_tsne.py` | t-SNE visualization |
| `scripts/exp40_llama_guard_baseline.py` | Llama Guard baseline |
| `scripts/exp46_inference_efficiency.py` | Inference efficiency benchmark |
| `scripts/exp53_llm_judge_optimized.py` | LLM-as-judge baseline |
| `scripts/exp57_svd_iid_only.py` | SVD subspace analysis |

## License

This project is for research purposes.
````

Domain Exposure Bridges the Cross-Attack Gap\n\nThis repository contains the code and experimental results for the paper *\"What Makes Multi-Turn Jailbreaks Detectable? Domain Exposure Bridges the Cross-Attack Gap\"*.\n\n## Overview\n\n**DisPeD** is a controlled framework that isolates how domain exposure, supervision type, and label semantics each contribute to out-of-distribution (OOD) robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection. The two-stage pipeline consists of:\n\n1. **Stage 1 — DeBERTa Multi-Task Fine-Tuning**: Fine-tunes DeBERTa-v3-base on per-turn persuasion strategy classification (9-class) with an auxiliary binary jailbreak intent head.\n2. **Stage 2 — GRU Sequence Classifier**: Trains a BiGRU classifier on conversation-level embeddings from the frozen Stage-1 encoder for binary jailbreak detection.\n\nThe resulting 184M-parameter pipeline achieves 24x speedup over LLM-as-judge alternatives.\n\n## Requirements\n\n- Python >= 3.10\n- PyTorch >= 2.0\n- CUDA-capable GPU (tested on A6000, L40)\n\nInstall dependencies:\n```bash\npip install torch torchvision torchaudio\npip install transformers datasets accelerate einops\npip install scikit-learn scipy tqdm\n```\n\n## Project Structure\n\n```\nsrc/\n├── data/\n│   ├── dataset.py          # TurnDataset & ConversationDataset\n│   └── collator.py         # Data collators for batching\n├── models/\n│   ├── deberta_multitask.py  # DeBERTa multi-task model (Stage 1)\n│   ├── gru_classifier.py     # BiGRU classifier (Stage 2)\n│   └── baseline.py           # Baseline model variants\n├── train_deberta.py         # Stage 1: DeBERTa multi-task training\n├── train_classifier.py      # Stage 2: GRU classifier training (9-class)\n├── train_classifier_binary.py # Stage 2: GRU classifier training (binary)\n├── evaluate.py              # Evaluation (F1, early detection, FPR@95TPR)\n├── train_deberta_mlm.py     # MLM continued pretraining control\n└── ...\nscripts/                     # Experiment scripts (data generation, ablations, analysis)\nresults/                     # Experiment result JSONs\ndocs/paper/                  # Paper source (LaTeX)\n```\n\n## Data Preparation\n\nThe training data consists of synthetic multi-turn conversations with per-turn persuasion strategy annotations. To generate the data:\n\n1. **Jailbreak conversations**: Generated using Crescendo-style multi-turn attacks with persuasion strategy labels.\n   ```bash\n   python scripts/generate_crescendo_v2.py\n   ```\n\n2. **Benign conversations**: Topic-anchored benign multi-turn dialogues.\n   ```bash\n   python src/generate_topic_anchored_benign_v3.py\n   ```\n\n3. **Merge and split**: Combine and create train/val/test splits.\n   ```bash\n   python src/merge_and_split.py\n   ```\n\nThe expected data format is JSONL with each line containing a conversation object with per-turn `strategy` labels and a conversation-level `label` field.\n\n## Training\n\n### Stage 1: DeBERTa Multi-Task Fine-Tuning (DAPT)\n\n```bash\npython src/train_deberta.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --val_data data/val.jsonl \\\n    --model_name microsoft/deberta-v3-base \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage1 \\\n    --epochs 5 \\\n    --batch_size 16 \\\n    --lr 2e-5\n```\n\n### Stage 2: GRU Sequence Classifier\n\n```bash\n# Treatment (persuasion-adapted encoder)\npython src/train_classifier.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --val_data data/val.jsonl \\\n    --mode treatment \\\n    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1/best \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage2_treatment \\\n    --epochs 30 \\\n    --batch_size 8\n\n# Baseline (vanilla DeBERTa encoder)\npython src/train_classifier.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --val_data data/val.jsonl \\\n    --mode baseline \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage2_baseline \\\n    --epochs 30 \\\n    --batch_size 8\n```\n\n### Evaluation\n\n```bash\npython src/evaluate.py \\\n    --test_data data/test.jsonl \\\n    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1/best \\\n    --gru_path checkpoints/stage2_treatment/best.pt \\\n    --output results/eval_results.json\n```\n\n## Key Experiments\n\nExperiment scripts are in `scripts/`. Notable ones:\n\n| Script | Description |\n|--------|-------------|\n| `scripts/exp3_probing.py` | Frozen probing analysis |\n| `scripts/exp28_data_efficiency.py` | Data efficiency ablation |\n| `scripts/exp34_representation_tsne.py` | t-SNE visualization |\n| `scripts/exp40_llama_guard_baseline.py` | Llama Guard baseline |\n| `scripts/exp46_inference_efficiency.py` | Inference efficiency benchmark |\n| `scripts/exp53_llm_judge_optimized.py` | LLM-as-judge baseline |\n| `scripts/exp57_svd_iid_only.py` | SVD subspace analysis |\n\n## License\n\nThis project is for research purposes.",

### Tool result

_source line 1365 · 2026-05-26T12:24:25.494Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014QVzqFUxHK7EQNPFDVQ85R`</summary>

```text
% abstract.tex
\begin{abstract}
Multi-turn jailbreaks spread adversarial intent across innocent-looking conversation turns, and safety classifiers designed for single-turn threats miss them: Llama Guard~3 catches only 1.8\%. What makes a detector generalize to attack strategies never seen during training? We introduce DisPeD (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), a controlled framework where seven encoder variants differ solely in pretraining objective while sharing the same frozen classifier; this design isolates how domain exposure, supervision type, and label semantics each contribute to out-of-distribution (OOD) robustness. Classification-based domain adaptation is the only consistently effective mechanism on synthetic multi-turn attacks: it recovers near-perfect detection where vanilla encoders collapse (F1 0.97--1.0 vs.\ 0.26--0.31) and remains robust under mixed-LLM training where unsupervised pretraining fails entirely. Scrambled labels match correct ones---the classification objective's structural role, not label content, drives generalization. Yet these advantages reverse on human-authored data, where unsupervised pretraining leads, an asymmetry that bounds the scope of each mechanism. The resulting 184M-parameter pipeline achieves 24$\times$ speedup over LLM-as-judge alternatives, and few-shot adaptation substantially reduces false-positive inflation across deployment platforms\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect-C207/}.}.
\end{abstract}
```

3:This repository contains the code and experimental results for the paper *"What Makes Multi-Turn Jailbreaks Detectable? Domain Exposure Bridges the Cross-Attack Gap"*.
5:## Overview
7:**DisPeD** is a controlled framework that isolates how domain exposure, supervision type, and label semantics each contribute to out-of-distribution (OOD) robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection. The two-stage pipeline consists of:
9:1. **Stage 1 — DeBERTa Multi-Task Fine-Tuning**: Fine-tunes DeBERTa-v3-base on per-turn persuasion strategy classification (9-class) with an auxiliary binary jailbreak intent head.
10:2. **Stage 2 — GRU Sequence Classifier**: Trains a BiGRU classifier on conversation-level embeddings from the frozen Stage-1 encoder for binary jailbreak detection.
35:│   ├── deberta_multitask.py  # DeBERTa multi-task model (Stage 1)
36:│   ├── gru_classifier.py     # BiGRU classifier (Stage 2)
37:│   └── baseline.py           # Baseline model variants
38:├── train_deberta.py         # Stage 1: DeBERTa multi-task training
39:├── train_classifier.py      # Stage 2: GRU classifier training (9-class)
40:├── train_classifier_binary.py # Stage 2: GRU classifier training (binary)
46:docs/paper/                  # Paper source (LaTeX)

93:    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1/best \

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JC6kQMCTnNXTsxgjdaYLYV`</summary>

```text
1	% method.tex
2	
3	\section{Method}
4	\label{sec:method}
5	
6	Cross-attack generalization could stem from domain exposure, classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, or domain specificity. Disentangling these factors demands an architecture where exactly one component varies across conditions. DisPeD separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2). Seven Stage-1 variants share the same training data and downstream pipeline but differ solely in their pretraining objective; each isolates a single factor (Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}).
7	
8	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
9	
10	A conversation $C = \{u_1, a_1, \ldots, u_N, a_N\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \in \{0, 1\}$ (jailbreak vs.\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.
11	
12	\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}
13	
14	Each user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\citep{he2023debertav3}; the \texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \in \mathbb{R}^D$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.
15	
16	\begin{table}[t]
17	\centering
18	\small
19	\caption{Stage-1 encoder variants. All operate on the same training conversations except Wikipedia MLM (matched token count on Wikipedia text).}
20	\label{tab:variants}
21	\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.6cm}p{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}
22	\toprule
23	\textbf{Variant} & \textbf{Objective} & \textbf{Tests whether\ldots} \\
24	\midrule
25	9-class & 9-class persuasion + binary intent & full model \\
26	Binary & binary persuasion + binary intent & granularity matters \\
27	Scrambled & random 9-class + binary intent & label correctness matters \\
28	MLM-only & 15\% token masking & supervision is necessary \\
29	Topic & 5-class topic classif. & any task suffices \\
30	Wiki MLM & 15\% masking on Wikipedia & domain specificity matters \\
31	Vanilla & no training & Stage-2 alone suffices \\
32	\bottomrule
33	\end{tabular}
34	\end{table}
35	
36	The \textbf{9-class} variant uses two classification heads: 9-class persuasion strategy classification ($L_{\text{strategy}}$) and binary jailbreak intent ($L_{\text{intent}}$), with combined loss $L = L_{\text{strategy}} + \alpha \cdot L_{\text{intent}}$ ($\alpha = 0.3$, selected from $\{0, 0.3, 0.5, 2.0\}$; all values yield F1\,=\,1.0 on IID; Appendix~\ref{app:lambda}). The taxonomy comprises eight persuasion strategies (rapport building, authority appeal, emotional manipulation, logical reframing, role assignment, gradual escalation, obfuscation, direct request) plus a none category, grounded in PAP~\citep{zeng2024pap} and Cialdini's principles~\citep{cialdini2001influence}. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting that includes definitions and examples for each category; inter-annotator agreement between model annotations and a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\kappa$=0.78.
37	
38	The \textbf{binary} variant collapses the 9-class taxonomy into persuasive vs.\ non-persuasive, testing whether label granularity matters for detection or robustness.
39	The \textbf{scrambled} variant randomly permutes the 9-class labels across turns using a fixed random permutation (seed 42), preserving classification structure (cross-entropy loss, 9-class output space) while destroying label semantics. This isolates whether the classification objective provides representational benefits independent of label correctness. Robustness is verified across additional random permutations with consistent results (Appendix~\ref{app:random_partition}).
40	The \textbf{MLM-only} variant applies 15\% masked language modeling on the same jailbreak conversation text without any classification head, testing whether unsupervised domain exposure suffices.
41	The \textbf{topic} variant trains a 5-class topic classifier (technology, health, finance, education, general) on the same conversations, testing whether \emph{any} classification task provides the same benefit or whether task alignment matters. This comparison confounds domain relevance with label granularity (5 vs.\ 9 classes) and classification-head architecture; the Wiki-MLM vs.\ JB-MLM pair provides a cleaner isolation of domain effects (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}).
42	The \textbf{Wikipedia MLM} variant applies the same MLM objective to a token-matched sample of Wikipedia text; this isolates domain-specific from general continued pretraining.
43	The \textbf{vanilla} baseline uses the pretrained DeBERTa-v3-base weights without any continued pretraining; it measures what Stage~2 alone can achieve.
44	
45	\subsection{Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification}
46	
47	Turn embeddings $\{h_1, \ldots, h_K\}$ from the frozen Stage-1 encoder are aggregated by a 2-layer bidirectional GRU ($d_{\text{hidden}}$=256, dropout 0.3): $\bar{h} = [\overrightarrow{h_K};\; \overleftarrow{h_1}] \in \mathbb{R}^{512}$.
48	A two-layer MLP ($512 \to 256 \to 2$, ReLU activation) maps $\bar{h}$ to $\hat{y} \in \{0, 1\}$.
49	The Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage~2 to ensure ablation fairness across all seven variants (freezing verification in Appendix~\ref{app:frozen_verify}; end-to-end fine-tuning comparison in Appendix~\ref{app:e2e}).
50	We retain BiGRU as the default aggregator for its amenability to representation analysis (Centered Kernel Alignment [CKA], t-SNE), noting that mean pooling achieves comparable detection (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Cross-architecture validation on RoBERTa (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) yields thirteen configurations across three scales.
```

{
  "file_path": "/tmp/persuasion_repo_sync/README.md",
  "content": "# DisPeD: A Controlled Study of Encoder Adaptation for Multi-Turn Jailbreak Detection\n\nThis repository contains the code and data for the paper *\"DisPeD: A Controlled Study of Encoder Adaptation for Multi-Turn Jailbreak Detection\"*.\n\n## Overview\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign conversation turns, evading single-turn safety classifiers. **DisPeD** (Disentangled Persuasion Detection) is a controlled framework that isolates how domain exposure, supervision type, and label semantics each contribute to out-of-distribution (OOD) robustness. The two-stage pipeline consists of:\n\n1. **Stage 1 — Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation**: Seven DeBERTa-v3-base encoder variants share the same training data but differ solely in their pretraining objective (9-class persuasion classification, binary, scrambled labels, MLM, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, or vanilla), each isolating a single factor.\n2. **Stage 2 — Conversation-Level Classification**: A frozen BiGRU aggregates [CLS] embeddings from the frozen Stage-1 encoder for binary jailbreak detection.\n\nKey findings:\n- Classification-based domain adaptation (DAPT) is the only consistently effective mechanism, recovering OOD detection where vanilla encoders collapse (F1 0.97--1.0 vs. 0.26--0.31).\n- Scrambled labels match correct labels — the classification objective's structural role, not label content, drives generalization.\n- These advantages reverse on human-authored data, where unsupervised pretraining leads, revealing condition-specific rather than universal advantages.\n- The 184M-parameter pipeline achieves 24x speedup over LLM-as-judge alternatives.\n\n## Requirements\n\n- Python >= 3.10\n- PyTorch >= 2.0\n- CUDA-capable GPU (tested on A6000, L40)\n\n```bash\npip install torch torchvision torchaudio\npip install transformers datasets accelerate\npip install scikit-learn scipy tqdm\n```\n\n## Project Structure\n\n```\nsrc/\n├── models/\n│   ├── deberta_multitask.py    # DeBERTa multi-task model (Stage 1)\n│   ├── deberta_topic.py        # Topic classification variant\n│   ├── gru_classifier.py       # BiGRU classifier (Stage 2)\n│   └── baseline.py             # Baseline model variants\n├── train_deberta.py            # Stage 1: 9-class DAPT training\n├── train_deberta_binary.py     # Stage 1: Binary variant\n├── train_deberta_mlm.py        # Stage 1: MLM continued pretraining\n├── train_deberta_mlm_wiki.py   # Stage 1: Wikipedia MLM control\n├── train_deberta_topic.py      # Stage 1: Topic classification control\n├── train_classifier.py         # Stage 2: BiGRU classifier training\n├── evaluate.py                 # Evaluation pipeline\n├── scramble_labels.py          # Label scrambling for scrambled variant\n└── ...\nscripts/                        # Experiment and analysis scripts\nresults/                        # Experiment result JSONs\ndocs/paper/                     # Paper source (LaTeX)\n```\n\n## Training\n\n### Stage 1: Encoder Adaptation (Seven Variants)\n\n**9-class DAPT** (primary variant):\n```bash\npython src/train_deberta.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --val_data data/val.jsonl \\\n    --model_name microsoft/deberta-v3-base \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage1_9class \\\n    --epochs 5 --batch_size 16 --lr 2e-5\n```\n\n**MLM-only** (unsupervised control):\n```bash\npython src/train_deberta_mlm.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --model_name microsoft/deberta-v3-base \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage1_mlm \\\n    --epochs 5 --batch_size 16\n```\n\n**Wikipedia MLM** (domain specificity control):\n```bash\npython src/train_deberta_mlm_wiki.py \\\n    --model_name microsoft/deberta-v3-base \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage1_wiki_mlm \\\n    --epochs 5 --batch_size 16\n```\n\n**Topic classification** (task alignment control):\n```bash\npython src/train_deberta_topic.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --model_name microsoft/deberta-v3-base \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage1_topic \\\n    --epochs 5 --batch_size 16\n```\n\n### Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification\n\n```bash\npython src/train_classifier.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --val_data data/val.jsonl \\\n    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1_9class/best \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage2 \\\n    --epochs 30 --batch_size 8\n```\n\n### Evaluation\n\n```bash\npython src/evaluate.py \\\n    --test_data data/test.jsonl \\\n    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1_9class/best \\\n    --gru_path checkpoints/stage2/best.pt \\\n    --output results/eval_results.json\n```\n\n## Data\n\nTraining data consists of synthetic multi-turn conversations with per-turn persuasion strategy annotations. Four attack families are used: Crescendo, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD), Deceptive Delight, and ActorAttack, generated via Qwen and Llama models. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B.\n\nThe expected data format is JSONL with each line containing a conversation object with per-turn `strategy` labels and a conversation-level `label` field.\n\n## License\n\nCC BY-NC 4.0\n"

````text
1	# What Makes Multi-Turn Jailbreaks Detectable? Domain Exposure Bridges the Cross-Attack Gap
2	
3	This repository contains the code and experimental results for the paper *"What Makes Multi-Turn Jailbreaks Detectable? Domain Exposure Bridges the Cross-Attack Gap"*.
4	
5	## Overview
6	
7	**DisPeD** is a controlled framework that isolates how domain exposure, supervision type, and label semantics each contribute to out-of-distribution (OOD) robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection. The two-stage pipeline consists of:
8	
9	1. **Stage 1 — DeBERTa Multi-Task Fine-Tuning**: Fine-tunes DeBERTa-v3-base on per-turn persuasion strategy classification (9-class) with an auxiliary binary jailbreak intent head.
10	2. **Stage 2 — GRU Sequence Classifier**: Trains a BiGRU classifier on conversation-level embeddings from the frozen Stage-1 encoder for binary jailbreak detection.
11	
12	The resulting 184M-parameter pipeline achieves 24x speedup over LLM-as-judge alternatives.
13	
14	## Requirements
15	
16	- Python >= 3.10
17	- PyTorch >= 2.0
18	- CUDA-capable GPU (tested on A6000, L40)
19	
20	Install dependencies:
21	```bash
22	pip install torch torchvision torchaudio
23	pip install transformers datasets accelerate einops
24	pip install scikit-learn scipy tqdm
25	```
26	
27	## Project Structure
28	
29	```
30	src/
31	├── data/
32	│   ├── dataset.py          # TurnDataset & ConversationDataset
33	│   └── collator.py         # Data collators for batching
34	├── models/
35	│   ├── deberta_multitask.py  # DeBERTa multi-task model (Stage 1)
36	│   ├── gru_classifier.py     # BiGRU classifier (Stage 2)
37	│   └── baseline.py           # Baseline model variants
38	├── train_deberta.py         # Stage 1: DeBERTa multi-task training
39	├── train_classifier.py      # Stage 2: GRU classifier training (9-class)
40	├── train_classifier_binary.py # Stage 2: GRU classifier training (binary)
41	├── evaluate.py              # Evaluation (F1, early detection, FPR@95TPR)
42	├── train_deberta_mlm.py     # MLM continued pretraining control
43	└── ...
44	scripts/                     # Experiment scripts (data generation, ablations, analysis)
45	results/                     # Experiment result JSONs
46	docs/paper/                  # Paper source (LaTeX)
47	```
48	
49	## Data Preparation
50	
51	The training data consists of synthetic multi-turn conversations with per-turn persuasion strategy annotations. To generate the data:
52	
53	1. **Jailbreak conversations**: Generated using Crescendo-style multi-turn attacks with persuasion strategy labels.
54	   ```bash
55	   python scripts/generate_crescendo_v2.py
56	   ```
57	
58	2. **Benign conversations**: Topic-anchored benign multi-turn dialogues.
59	   ```bash
60	   python src/generate_topic_anchored_benign_v3.py
61	   ```
62	
63	3. **Merge and split**: Combine and create train/val/test splits.
64	   ```bash
65	   python src/merge_and_split.py
66	   ```
67	
68	The expected data format is JSONL with each line containing a conversation object with per-turn `strategy` labels and a conversation-level `label` field.
69	
70	## Training
71	
72	### Stage 1: DeBERTa Multi-Task Fine-Tuning (DAPT)
73	
74	```bash
75	python src/train_deberta.py \
76	    --train_data data/train.jsonl \
77	    --val_data data/val.jsonl \
78	    --model_name microsoft/deberta-v3-base \
79	    --output_dir checkpoints/stage1 \
80	    --epochs 5 \
81	    --batch_size 16 \
82	    --lr 2e-5
83	```
84	
85	### Stage 2: GRU Sequence Classifier
86	
87	```bash
88	# Treatment (persuasion-adapted encoder)
89	python src/train_classifier.py \
90	    --train_data data/train.jsonl \
91	    --val_data data/val.jsonl \
92	    --mode treatment \
93	    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1/best \
94	    --output_dir checkpoints/stage2_treatment \
95	    --epochs 30 \
96	    --batch_size 8
97	
98	# Baseline (vanilla DeBERTa encoder)
99	python src/train_classifier.py \
100	    --train_data data/train.jsonl \
101	    --val_data data/val.jsonl \
102	    --mode baseline \
103	    --output_dir checkpoints/stage2_baseline \
104	    --epochs 30 \
105	    --batch_size 8
106	```
107	
108	### Evaluation
109	
110	```bash
111	python src/evaluate.py \
112	    --test_data data/test.jsonl \
113	    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1/best \
114	    --gru_path checkpoints/stage2_treatment/best.pt \
115	    --output results/eval_results.json
116	```
117	
118	## Key Experiments
119	
120	Experiment scripts are in `scripts/`. Notable ones:
121	
122	| Script | Description |
123	|--------|-------------|
124	| `scripts/exp3_probing.py` | Frozen probing analysis |
125	| `scripts/exp28_data_efficiency.py` | Data efficiency ablation |
126	| `scripts/exp34_representation_tsne.py` | t-SNE visualization |
127	| `scripts/exp40_llama_guard_baseline.py` | Llama Guard baseline |
128	| `scripts/exp46_inference_efficiency.py` | Inference efficiency benchmark |
129	| `scripts/exp53_llm_judge_optimized.py` | LLM-as-judge baseline |
130	| `scripts/exp57_svd_iid_only.py` | SVD subspace analysis |
131	
132	## License
133	
134	This project is for research purposes.
135	
````

Domain Exposure Bridges the Cross-Attack Gap\n\nThis repository contains the code and experimental results for the paper *\"What Makes Multi-Turn Jailbreaks Detectable? Domain Exposure Bridges the Cross-Attack Gap\"*.\n\n## Overview\n\n**DisPeD** is a controlled framework that isolates how domain exposure, supervision type, and label semantics each contribute to out-of-distribution (OOD) robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection. The two-stage pipeline consists of:\n\n1. **Stage 1 — DeBERTa Multi-Task Fine-Tuning**: Fine-tunes DeBERTa-v3-base on per-turn persuasion strategy classification (9-class) with an auxiliary binary jailbreak intent head.\n2. **Stage 2 — GRU Sequence Classifier**: Trains a BiGRU classifier on conversation-level embeddings from the frozen Stage-1 encoder for binary jailbreak detection.\n\nThe resulting 184M-parameter pipeline achieves 24x speedup over LLM-as-judge alternatives.\n\n## Requirements\n\n- Python >= 3.10\n- PyTorch >= 2.0\n- CUDA-capable GPU (tested on A6000, L40)\n\nInstall dependencies:\n```bash\npip install torch torchvision torchaudio\npip install transformers datasets accelerate einops\npip install scikit-learn scipy tqdm\n```\n\n## Project Structure\n\n```\nsrc/\n├── data/\n│   ├── dataset.py          # TurnDataset & ConversationDataset\n│   └── collator.py         # Data collators for batching\n├── models/\n│   ├── deberta_multitask.py  # DeBERTa multi-task model (Stage 1)\n│   ├── gru_classifier.py     # BiGRU classifier (Stage 2)\n│   └── baseline.py           # Baseline model variants\n├── train_deberta.py         # Stage 1: DeBERTa multi-task training\n├── train_classifier.py      # Stage 2: GRU classifier training (9-class)\n├── train_classifier_binary.py # Stage 2: GRU classifier training (binary)\n├── evaluate.py              # Evaluation (F1, early detection, FPR@95TPR)\n├── train_deberta_mlm.py     # MLM continued pretraining control\n└── ...\nscripts/                     # Experiment scripts (data generation, ablations, analysis)\nresults/                     # Experiment result JSONs\ndocs/paper/                  # Paper source (LaTeX)\n```\n\n## Data Preparation\n\nThe training data consists of synthetic multi-turn conversations with per-turn persuasion strategy annotations. To generate the data:\n\n1. **Jailbreak conversations**: Generated using Crescendo-style multi-turn attacks with persuasion strategy labels.\n   ```bash\n   python scripts/generate_crescendo_v2.py\n   ```\n\n2. **Benign conversations**: Topic-anchored benign multi-turn dialogues.\n   ```bash\n   python src/generate_topic_anchored_benign_v3.py\n   ```\n\n3. **Merge and split**: Combine and create train/val/test splits.\n   ```bash\n   python src/merge_and_split.py\n   ```\n\nThe expected data format is JSONL with each line containing a conversation object with per-turn `strategy` labels and a conversation-level `label` field.\n\n## Training\n\n### Stage 1: DeBERTa Multi-Task Fine-Tuning (DAPT)\n\n```bash\npython src/train_deberta.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --val_data data/val.jsonl \\\n    --model_name microsoft/deberta-v3-base \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage1 \\\n    --epochs 5 \\\n    --batch_size 16 \\\n    --lr 2e-5\n```\n\n### Stage 2: GRU Sequence Classifier\n\n```bash\n# Treatment (persuasion-adapted encoder)\npython src/train_classifier.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --val_data data/val.jsonl \\\n    --mode treatment \\\n    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1/best \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage2_treatment \\\n    --epochs 30 \\\n    --batch_size 8\n\n# Baseline (vanilla DeBERTa encoder)\npython src/train_classifier.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --val_data data/val.jsonl \\\n    --mode baseline \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage2_baseline \\\n    --epochs 30 \\\n    --batch_size 8\n```\n\n### Evaluation\n\n```bash\npython src/evaluate.py \\\n    --test_data data/test.jsonl \\\n    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1/best \\\n    --gru_path checkpoints/stage2_treatment/best.pt \\\n    --output results/eval_results.json\n```\n\n## Key Experiments\n\nExperiment scripts are in `scripts/`. Notable ones:\n\n| Script | Description |\n|--------|-------------|\n| `scripts/exp3_probing.py` | Frozen probing analysis |\n| `scripts/exp28_data_efficiency.py` | Data efficiency ablation |\n| `scripts/exp34_representation_tsne.py` | t-SNE visualization |\n| `scripts/exp40_llama_guard_baseline.py` | Llama Guard baseline |\n| `scripts/exp46_inference_efficiency.py` | Inference efficiency benchmark |\n| `scripts/exp53_llm_judge_optimized.py` | LLM-as-judge baseline |\n| `scripts/exp57_svd_iid_only.py` | SVD subspace analysis |\n\n## License\n\nThis project is for research purposes.\n",

{
  "file_path": "/tmp/persuasion_repo_sync/README.md",
  "content": "# DisPeD: A Controlled Study of Encoder Adaptation for Multi-Turn Jailbreak Detection\n\nThis repository contains the code and data for the paper *\"DisPeD: A Controlled Study of Encoder Adaptation for Multi-Turn Jailbreak Detection\"*.\n\n## Overview\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign conversation turns, evading single-turn safety classifiers. **DisPeD** (Disentangled Persuasion Detection) is a controlled framework that isolates how domain exposure, supervision type, and label semantics each contribute to out-of-distribution (OOD) robustness. The two-stage pipeline consists of:\n\n1. **Stage 1 — Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation**: Seven DeBERTa-v3-base encoder variants share the same training data but differ solely in their pretraining objective (9-class persuasion classification, binary, scrambled labels, MLM, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, or vanilla), each isolating a single factor.\n2. **Stage 2 — Conversation-Level Classification**: A frozen BiGRU aggregates [CLS] embeddings from the frozen Stage-1 encoder for binary jailbreak detection.\n\nKey findings:\n- Classification-based domain adaptation (DAPT) is the only consistently effective mechanism, recovering OOD detection where vanilla encoders collapse (F1 0.97--1.0 vs. 0.26--0.31).\n- Scrambled labels match correct labels -- the classification objective's structural role, not label content, drives generalization.\n- These advantages reverse on human-authored data, where unsupervised pretraining leads, revealing condition-specific rather than universal advantages.\n- The 184M-parameter pipeline achieves 24x speedup over LLM-as-judge alternatives.\n\n## Requirements\n\n- Python >= 3.10\n- PyTorch >= 2.0\n- CUDA-capable GPU (tested on A6000, L40)\n\n```bash\npip install torch torchvision torchaudio\npip install transformers datasets accelerate\npip install scikit-learn scipy tqdm\n```\n\n## Project Structure\n\n```\nsrc/\n├── models/\n│   ├── deberta_multitask.py    # DeBERTa multi-task model (Stage 1)\n│   ├── deberta_topic.py        # Topic classification variant\n│   ├── gru_classifier.py       # BiGRU classifier (Stage 2)\n│   └── baseline.py             # Baseline model variants\n├── train_deberta.py            # Stage 1: 9-class DAPT training\n├── train_deberta_binary.py     # Stage 1: Binary variant\n├── train_deberta_mlm.py        # Stage 1: MLM continued pretraining\n├── train_deberta_mlm_wiki.py   # Stage 1: Wikipedia MLM control\n├── train_deberta_topic.py      # Stage 1: Topic classification control\n├── train_classifier.py         # Stage 2: BiGRU classifier training\n├── evaluate.py                 # Evaluation pipeline\n├── scramble_labels.py          # Label scrambling for scrambled variant\n└── ...\nscripts/                        # Experiment and analysis scripts\nresults/                        # Experiment result JSONs\ndocs/paper/                     # Paper source (LaTeX)\n```\n\n## Training\n\n### Stage 1: Encoder Adaptation (Seven Variants)\n\n**9-class DAPT** (primary variant):\n```bash\npython src/train_deberta.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --val_data data/val.jsonl \\\n    --model_name microsoft/deberta-v3-base \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage1_9class \\\n    --epochs 5 --batch_size 16 --lr 2e-5\n```\n\n**MLM-only** (unsupervised control):\n```bash\npython src/train_deberta_mlm.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --model_name microsoft/deberta-v3-base \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage1_mlm \\\n    --epochs 5 --batch_size 16\n```\n\n**Wikipedia MLM** (domain specificity control):\n```bash\npython src/train_deberta_mlm_wiki.py \\\n    --model_name microsoft/deberta-v3-base \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage1_wiki_mlm \\\n    --epochs 5 --batch_size 16\n```\n\n**Topic classification** (task alignment control):\n```bash\npython src/train_deberta_topic.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --model_name microsoft/deberta-v3-base \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage1_topic \\\n    --epochs 5 --batch_size 16\n```\n\n### Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification\n\n```bash\npython src/train_classifier.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --val_data data/val.jsonl \\\n    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1_9class/best \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage2 \\\n    --epochs 30 --batch_size 8\n```\n\n### Evaluation\n\n```bash\npython src/evaluate.py \\\n    --test_data data/test.jsonl \\\n    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1_9class/best \\\n    --gru_path checkpoints/stage2/best.pt \\\n    --output results/eval_results.json\n```\n\n## Data\n\nTraining data consists of synthetic multi-turn conversations with per-turn persuasion strategy annotations. Four attack families are used: Crescendo, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD), Deceptive Delight, and ActorAttack, generated via Qwen and Llama models. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B.\n\nThe expected data format is JSONL with each line containing a conversation object with per-turn `strategy` labels and a conversation-level `label` field.\n\n## License\n\nCC BY-NC 4.0\n"
}

          "-1. **Stage 1 — DeBERTa Multi-Task Fine-Tuning**: Fine-tunes DeBERTa-v3-base on per-turn persuasion strategy classification (9-class) with an auxiliary binary jailbreak intent head.",
          "-2. **Stage 2 — GRU Sequence Classifier**: Trains a BiGRU classifier on conversation-level embeddings from the frozen Stage-1 encoder for binary jailbreak detection.",
          "+1. **Stage 1 — Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation**: Seven DeBERTa-v3-base encoder variants share the same training data but differ solely in their pretraining objective (9-class persuasion classification, binary, scrambled labels, MLM, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, or vanilla), each isolating a single factor.",
          "+2. **Stage 2 — Conversation-Level Classification**: A frozen BiGRU aggregates [CLS] embeddings from the frozen Stage-1 encoder for binary jailbreak detection.",
          " ",
          "-The resulting 184M-parameter pipeline achieves 24x speedup over LLM-as-judge alternatives.",
          "+Key findings:",
          "+- Classification-based domain adaptation (DAPT) is the only consistently effective mechanism, recovering OOD detection where vanilla encoders collapse (F1 0.97--1.0 vs. 0.26--0.31).",
          "+- Scrambled labels match correct labels -- the classification objective's structural role, not label content, drives generalization.",
          "+- These advantages reverse on human-authored data, where unsupervised pretraining leads, revealing condition-specific rather than universal advantages.",
          "+- The 184M-parameter pipeline achieves 24x speedup over LLM-as-judge alternatives.",
          " ",
          " ## Requirements",
          " "
        ]

Four attack families are used: Crescendo, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD), Deceptive Delight, and ActorAttack, generated via Qwen and Llama models. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B.",

    "originalFile": "# What Makes Multi-Turn Jailbreaks Detectable? Domain Exposure Bridges the Cross-Attack Gap\n\nThis repository contains the code and experimental results for the paper *\"What Makes Multi-Turn Jailbreaks Detectable? Domain Exposure Bridges the Cross-Attack Gap\"*.\n\n## Overview\n\n**DisPeD** is a controlled framework that isolates how domain exposure, supervision type, and label semantics each contribute to out-of-distribution (OOD) robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection. The two-stage pipeline consists of:\n\n1. **Stage 1 — DeBERTa Multi-Task Fine-Tuning**: Fine-tunes DeBERTa-v3-base on per-turn persuasion strategy classification (9-class) with an auxiliary binary jailbreak intent head.\n2. **Stage 2 — GRU Sequence Classifier**: Trains a BiGRU classifier on conversation-level embeddings from the frozen Stage-1 encoder for binary jailbreak detection.\n\nThe resulting 184M-parameter pipeline achieves 24x speedup over LLM-as-judge alternatives.\n\n## Requirements\n\n- Python >= 3.10\n- PyTorch >= 2.0\n- CUDA-capable GPU (tested on A6000, L40)\n\nInstall dependencies:\n```bash\npip install torch torchvision torchaudio\npip install transformers datasets accelerate einops\npip install scikit-learn scipy tqdm\n```\n\n## Project Structure\n\n```\nsrc/\n├── data/\n│   ├── dataset.py          # TurnDataset & ConversationDataset\n│   └── collator.py         # Data collators for batching\n├── models/\n│   ├── deberta_multitask.py  # DeBERTa multi-task model (Stage 1)\n│   ├── gru_classifier.py     # BiGRU classifier (Stage 2)\n│   └── baseline.py           # Baseline model variants\n├── train_deberta.py         # Stage 1: DeBERTa multi-task training\n├── train_classifier.py      # Stage 2: GRU classifier training (9-class)\n├── train_classifier_binary.py # Stage 2: GRU classifier training (binary)\n├── evaluate.py              # Evaluation (F1, early detection, FPR@95TPR)\n├── train_deberta_mlm.py     # MLM continued pretraining control\n└── ...\nscripts/                     # Experiment scripts (data generation, ablations, analysis)\nresults/                     # Experiment result JSONs\ndocs/paper/                  # Paper source (LaTeX)\n```\n\n## Data Preparation\n\nThe training data consists of synthetic multi-turn conversations with per-turn persuasion strategy annotations. To generate the data:\n\n1. **Jailbreak conversations**: Generated using Crescendo-style multi-turn attacks with persuasion strategy labels.\n   ```bash\n   python scripts/generate_crescendo_v2.py\n   ```\n\n2. **Benign conversations**: Topic-anchored benign multi-turn dialogues.\n   ```bash\n   python src/generate_topic_anchored_benign_v3.py\n   ```\n\n3. **Merge and split**: Combine and create train/val/test splits.\n   ```bash\n   python src/merge_and_split.py\n   ```\n\nThe expected data format is JSONL with each line containing a conversation object with per-turn `strategy` labels and a conversation-level `label` field.\n\n## Training\n\n### Stage 1: DeBERTa Multi-Task Fine-Tuning (DAPT)\n\n```bash\npython src/train_deberta.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --val_data data/val.jsonl \\\n    --model_name microsoft/deberta-v3-base \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage1 \\\n    --epochs 5 \\\n    --batch_size 16 \\\n    --lr 2e-5\n```\n\n### Stage 2: GRU Sequence Classifier\n\n```bash\n# Treatment (persuasion-adapted encoder)\npython src/train_classifier.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --val_data data/val.jsonl \\\n    --mode treatment \\\n    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1/best \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage2_treatment \\\n    --epochs 30 \\\n    --batch_size 8\n\n# Baseline (vanilla DeBERTa encoder)\npython src/train_classifier.py \\\n    --train_data data/train.jsonl \\\n    --val_data data/val.jsonl \\\n    --mode baseline \\\n    --output_dir checkpoints/stage2_baseline \\\n    --epochs 30 \\\n    --batch_size 8\n```\n\n### Evaluation\n\n```bash\npython src/evaluate.py \\\n    --test_data data/test.jsonl \\\n    --deberta_path checkpoints/stage1/best \\\n    --gru_path checkpoints/stage2_treatment/best.pt \\\n    --output results/eval_results.json\n```\n\n## Key Experiments\n\nExperiment scripts are in `scripts/`. Notable ones:\n\n| Script | Description |\n|--------|-------------|\n| `scripts/exp3_probing.py` | Frozen probing analysis |\n| `scripts/exp28_data_efficiency.py` | Data efficiency ablation |\n| `scripts/exp34_representation_tsne.py` | t-SNE visualization |\n| `scripts/exp40_llama_guard_baseline.py` | Llama Guard baseline |\n| `scripts/exp46_inference_efficiency.py` | Inference efficiency benchmark |\n| `scripts/exp53_llm_judge_optimized.py` | LLM-as-judge baseline |\n| `scripts/exp57_svd_iid_only.py` | SVD subspace analysis |\n\n## License\n\nThis project is for research purposes.\n",
    "userModified": false
  },
